## ![](_page_0_Picture_0.jpeg)

# **Fascism**

**Fascism** (/ˈfæʃɪzə[m/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/English) *[FASH](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:Pronunciation_respelling_key)-iz-*ə*m*) is a [far-right,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far-right) [authoritarian,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authoritarian) and [ultranationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ultranationalist) political ideology and movement that rose to prominence in early-20th-century Europe.[\[1\]](#page-31-0)[\[2\]](#page-32-0)[\[3\]](#page-32-1) Fascism is characterized by support for a [dictatorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dictator) leader, centralized [autocracy,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autocracy) [militarism,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Militarism) forcible suppression of [opposition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opposition_(politics)), belief in a natural [social](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_hierarchy) [hierarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_hierarchy), subordination of [individual](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Individual) interests for the perceived interest of the [nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nation) or [race,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Race_(human_categorization)) and strong regimentation of society and the economy. [\[3\]](#page-32-1)[\[4\]](#page-32-2) Opposed to [communism,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communism) [democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy), [liberalism,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberalism) [pluralism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pluralism_(political_philosophy)), and [socialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialism), [\[5\]](#page-32-3)[\[6\]](#page-32-4) fascism is at the far-right of the traditional [left–right spectrum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Left%E2%80%93right_spectrum). [\[1\]](#page-31-0)[\[6\]](#page-32-4)[\[7\]](#page-32-5)

The first fascist movements emerged [in Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_fascism) during [World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_I) [War I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_I) before spreading to [other European countries,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fascism_in_Europe) most notably [Germany.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_Germany) [\[1\]](#page-31-0) Fascism also had adherents outside of Europe.[\[8\]](#page-32-6) Fascists saw World War I as a [revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolution) that

## ![](_page_0_Picture_4.jpeg)

Benito [Mussolini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benito_Mussolini), dictator of [Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Italy) (left), and [Adolf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adolf_Hitler) Hitler, dictator of [Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_Germany) (right), were notable fascist leaders.

brought massive changes to the nature of war, society, the state, and technology. The advent of [total war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Total_war) and the [mass mobilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_mobilization) of society erased the distinction between civilians and combatants. A military citizenship arose, in which all citizens were involved with the military in some manner. [\[9\]](#page-32-7) The war resulted in the rise of a powerful state capable of mobilizing millions of people to serve on the front lines, providing logistics to support them, and having unprecedented authority to intervene in the lives of citizens.[\[9\]](#page-32-7)

Fascism views forms of violence—including [political violence,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_violence) [imperialist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperialist) violence, and [war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War)—as means to national rejuvenation.[\[10\]](#page-32-8)[\[11\]](#page-32-9) Fascists often advocate for the establishment of a [totalitarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Totalitarian) [one-party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One-party_state) [state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One-party_state), [\[12\]](#page-32-10)[\[13\]](#page-32-11) and for a [dirigiste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dirigiste) economy, which is a [market economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_economy) in which the state plays a strong directive role through [market intervention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_intervention) with the principal goal of achieving national economic selfsufficiency, or "[autarky.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autarky)"[\[14\]](#page-32-12)[\[15\]](#page-32-13) Fascism emphasizes both [palingenesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palingenesis)—national rebirth or regeneration —and [modernity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modernity) when it is deemed compatible with national rebirth.[\[16\]](#page-32-14) In promoting the nation's regeneration, fascists seek to purge it of decadence.[\[16\]](#page-32-14) Fascism may also centre around an [ingroup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/In-group_and_out-group)[outgroup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/In-group_and_out-group) opposition and [demonization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demonization) of "[Others"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Other_(philosophy)) such as various [ethnicities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnicity), [immigrants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration), nations, races, political opponents of fascist parties, religious groups, and [sexual and gender minorities.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_and_gender_minorities) In the case of [Nazism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazism), this involved [racial purity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_purity) and a belief in a [master race.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Master_race) Such demonization has motivated fascist regimes to commit [massacres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massacre), [forced sterilizations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_sterilizations), [deportations,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deportation) and [genocides.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genocide) [\[17\]](#page-32-15)[\[18\]](#page-32-16) During [World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_II) [II,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_II) the genocidal and imperialist ambitions of the fascist regimes of the [Axis powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Axis_powers) resulted in the murder of millions of people.

Since the end of World War II in 1945, fascism has been largely disgraced, and few parties have openly described themselves as *fascist*; the term is often [used pejoratively](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fascist_(insult)) by political opponents. The descriptions *[neo-fascist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-fascist)* or *[post-fascist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-fascist)* are sometimes applied to contemporary parties with ideologies similar to, or rooted in, 20th-century fascist movements.[\[1\]](#page-31-0)[\[19\]](#page-32-18)

# **Etymology**

The Italian term *fascismo* is derived from *fascio*, meaning 'bundle of sticks', ultimately from the [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_language) word *[fasces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fasces)*. [\[4\]](#page-32-2) This was the name given to political organizations in Italy known as [fasci,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fasci) groups similar to [guilds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_guilds) or [syndicates.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syndicate) According to Italian fascist dictator [Benito Mussolini'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benito_Mussolini)s own account, the [Fasces of Revolutionary Action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fasci_d%27Azione_Rivoluzionaria) were founded in Italy in 1915.[\[21\]](#page-32-19) In 1919, Mussolini founded the [Italian Fasces of Combat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fasci_Italiani_di_Combattimento) in Milan, which became the [National Fascist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Fascist_Party) two years later. The fascists came to associate the term with the ancient Roman fasces or *[fascio littorio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fascio_littorio)*, [\[22\]](#page-32-20) a bundle of rods tied around an axe,[\[23\]](#page-33-0) an [ancient](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Roman) [Roman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Roman) symbol of the authority of the civic [magistrate,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Magistrates) [\[24\]](#page-33-1) carried by his [lictors.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lictor) [\[25\]](#page-33-2) The symbolism of the fasces suggested strength through unity: a single rod is easily broken, while the bundle is difficult to break.[\[26\]](#page-33-3)

Prior to 1914, the fasces symbol was widely employed by various political movements, often of a left-wing or liberal persuasion. For instance, according to Robert Paxton, ["Marianne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marianne), symbol of the French Republic, was often portrayed in the nineteenth century carrying the

## ![](_page_1_Picture_4.jpeg)

The [fasces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fasces), a symbol of [Ancient](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Rome) Rome, was employed in the modern era by various political movements to denote strength through unity.

fasces to represent the force of Republican solidarity against her aristocratic and clerical enemies."[\[20\]](#page-32-17) The symbol often appeared as an architectural motif, for instance on the [Sheldonian Theater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheldonian_Theatre) at Oxford University and on the [Lincoln Memorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lincoln_Memorial) in Washington, D. C.[\[20\]](#page-32-17)

# **Definitions**

Historian [Ian Kershaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ian_Kershaw) once wrote, "Trying to define 'fascism' is like trying to nail jelly to the wall."[\[27\]](#page-33-4) Each group described as "fascist" has at least some unique elements, and frequently definitions of "fascism" have been criticized as either too broad or too narrow. [\[28\]](#page-33-5) According to many scholars, fascists —especially when they are in power—have historically attacked communism, socialism, and parliamentary liberalism, attracting support primarily from the far-right.[\[29\]](#page-33-6)

One of the schools of fascism studies understands fascism as a movement based on the myth of national rebirth, called [palingenesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palingenesis). [\[30\]](#page-33-7) Prominent members of the school include [Stanley G. Payne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stanley_G._Payne), [Roger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roger_Griffin) [Griffin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roger_Griffin), and [Roger Eatwell,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roger_Eatwell) who defined their theories as the "new consensus".[\[31\]](#page-33-8) Payne's definition of fascism focuses on three concepts:[\[32\]](#page-33-9)

- 1. "Fascist negations" [anti-liberalism,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-liberalism) [anti-communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-communism), and anti-[conservatism.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservatism)
- 2. "Fascist goals" the creation of a nationalist [dictatorship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dictatorship) to regulate economic structure and to transform social relations within a modern, self-determined culture, and the expansion of the nation into an empire.

3. "Fascist style" – a political aesthetic of romantic symbolism, mass mobilization, a positive view of violence, and promotion of masculinity, youth, and charismatic authoritarian leadership.[\[33\]](#page-33-10)

Eatwell defines fascism as "an ideology that strives to forge social rebirth based on a [holistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holism)-national radical [Third Way"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Position).[\[34\]](#page-33-11) Roger Griffin follows the description of Payne, calling fascism "a genuinely revolutionary, trans-class form of anti-liberal, and in the last analysis, anti-conservative nationalism", and adds an emphasis[\[35\]](#page-33-12)[\[31\]](#page-33-8) on the "mythic core" of fascism which he defines as a "palingenetic form of populist [ultranationalism"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ultranationalism).[\[36\]](#page-33-13) According to Griffin, fascism as an ideology includes: "(i) the rebirth myth, (ii) populist ultra-nationalism, and (iii) the myth of decadence."[\[37\]](#page-33-14) Thus, [palingenetic ultranationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palingenetic_ultranationalism) constitutes the minimum without which a "genuine fascism" is not possible according to Griffin,[\[38\]](#page-33-15)[\[39\]](#page-33-16) and fascism draws on ancient and arcane myths of racial, cultural, ethnic, and national origins to develop the fascist ["new man"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Man_(utopian_concept))' " [\[40\]](#page-33-17) and acts as a "political religion" seeking to establish a community based on a new culture.[\[39\]](#page-33-16) Griffin explored this 'mythic' or 'eliminable' core of fascism with his concept of *postfascism* to explore the continuation of Nazism in the modern era.[\[41\]](#page-33-18) Additionally, other historians have applied this minimalist core to explore *proto-fascist* movements.[\[42\]](#page-33-19)[\[43\]](#page-33-20)

While the theories of "new consensus" authors have proven to be very influential, they have also failed to establish a historiographic consensus and received criticism from other scholars, often as a static and isolated from context "[ideal type"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ideal_type) or set of ideas disregarding the dynamic, contradictory and syncretic nature of fascism and its context.[\[39\]](#page-33-16)[\[44\]](#page-33-21)[\[45\]](#page-33-22) In particular, the critics pointed out the failure to address the contradiction between anti-conservatism and anti-communism, since fascism preserved the latter while achieving power through alliance conservatism, or that the majority of fascists sought to synthesize fascism with traditional religions and cultures instead of replacing them.[\[46\]](#page-33-23)[\[39\]](#page-33-16) Griffin's 'minimal' criterion of national rebirth also received criticism as too broad, applicable to non-fascist movements,[\[39\]](#page-33-16) or arbitrary, not applicable to certain regimes which may be considered fascist,[\[47\]](#page-33-24)[\[45\]](#page-33-22) and also as a rework of the '[totalitarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Totalitarianism#Nazism_and_Fascism)' model of understanding fascism.[\[39\]](#page-33-16)

[Walter Laqueur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_Laqueur) sees the core tenets of fascism as "self-evident: nationalism; [social Darwinism;](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Darwinism) racialism, the need for leadership, a new aristocracy, and obedience; and the negation of the ideals of the Enlightenment and the French Revolution."[\[48\]](#page-33-25)

Kershaw argues that the difference between fascism and other forms of right-wing authoritarianism in the [interwar period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interwar_period) is that the latter generally aimed "to conserve the existing social order", whereas fascism was "revolutionary", seeking to change society and obtain "total commitment" from the population.[\[49\]](#page-33-26)

In his book *[How Fascism Works: The Politics of Us and Them](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/How_Fascism_Works:_The_Politics_of_Us_and_Them)* (2018), [Jason Stanley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jason_Stanley) defined fascism thusly:

[A] cult of the leader who promises national restoration in the face of humiliation brought on by supposed communists, Marxists and minorities and immigrants who are supposedly posing a threat to the character and the history of a nation ... The leader proposes that only he can solve it and all of his political opponents are enemies or traitors.

Stanley says recent global events as of 2020, including the [COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic) and the [2020–2023](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020%E2%80%932023_United_States_racial_unrest) [United States racial unrest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020%E2%80%932023_United_States_racial_unrest), have substantiated his concern about how fascist rhetoric is showing up in politics and policies around the world.[\[50\]](#page-33-27)

[Cas Mudde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cas_Mudde) and Cristóbal Rovira Kaltwasser argue that although fascism "flirted with populism ... in an attempt to generate mass support", it is better seen as an elitist ideology. [\[51\]](#page-33-28) They cite in particular its exaltation of the Leader, the race, and the state, rather than the people. They see populism as a "thincentered ideology" with a "restricted morphology" that necessarily becomes attached to "thick-centered" ideologies such as fascism, liberalism, or socialism. Thus populism can be found as an aspect of many specific ideologies, without necessarily being a defining characteristic of those ideologies. They refer to the combination of populism, authoritarianism and ultranationalism as "a marriage of convenience".[\[52\]](#page-34-0)

#### [Robert Paxton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Paxton) says:

[Fascism is] a form of political behavior marked by obsessive preoccupation with community decline, humiliation, or victimhood and by compensatory cults of unity, energy, and purity, in which a mass-based party of committed nationalist militants, working in uneasy but effective collaboration with traditional elites, abandons democratic liberties and pursues with redemptive violence and without ethical or legal restraints goals of internal cleansing and external expansion.[\[53\]](#page-34-1)

[Umberto Eco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umberto_Eco) lists fourteen "features that are typical of what [he] would like to call 'Ur-Fascism', or 'Eternal Fascism'. These features cannot be organized into a system; many of them contradict each other, and are also typical of other kinds of [despotism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Despotism) or fanaticism. But it is enough that one of them be present to allow fascism to coagulate around it."[\[54\]](#page-34-2) Historian [John Lukacs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Lukacs) argues that there is no such thing as generic fascism. He claims that [Nazism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazism) and [communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communism) are essentially manifestations of [populism,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Populism) and that states such as Nazi Germany and [Fascist Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fascist_Italy_(1922%E2%80%931943)) are more different from each other than they are similar. [\[55\]](#page-34-3)

#### Historian [Emilio Gentile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emilio_Gentile) has defined fascism thusly:

[A] modern political phenomenon, [revolutionary,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolutionary) [anti-liberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-liberal), and [anti-Marxist,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Marxist) organized in a militia party with a [totalitarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Totalitarian) conception of politics and the state, an activist and antitheoretical ideology, with a mythical, virilistic and anti-hedonistic foundation, sacralized as a secular religion, which affirms the absolute primacy of the nation, understood as an ethnically homogeneous organic community, hierarchically organized in a [corporate state,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corporate_state) with a bellicose vocation to the politics of greatness, power, and conquest aimed at creating a new order and a new civilization.[\[56\]](#page-34-4)

Historian and cultural critic [Ruth Ben-Ghiat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruth_Ben-Ghiat) has described fascism as "the original phase of authoritarianism, along with early communism, when a population has undergone huge dislocations or they perceive that there's been changes in society that are very rapid, too rapid for their taste".[\[57\]](#page-34-5)

Racism was a key feature of German fascism, for which [the Holocaust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Holocaust) was a high priority. According to *The Historiography of Genocide*, "In dealing with the Holocaust, it is the consensus of historians that Nazi Germany targeted Jews as a race, not as a religious group."[\[58\]](#page-34-6) Several historians, such as Umberto Eco,[\[54\]](#page-34-2) Kevin Passmore,[\[59\]](#page-34-7) and Moyra Grant,[\[60\]](#page-34-8) stress [racism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racism) as a characteristic component of German fascism. Historian [Robert Soucy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Soucy) stated, "Hitler envisioned the ideal German society as a *[Volksgemeinschaft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volksgemeinschaft)*, a racially unified and hierarchically organized body in which the interests of individuals would be strictly subordinate to those of the nation, or Volk."[\[61\]](#page-34-9) Kershaw noted that common factors of fascism included "the 'cleansing' of all those deemed not to belong—foreigners, ethnic minorities, 'undesirables'"—and belief in its own nation's superiority, even if it was not biological racism like in Nazism.[\[49\]](#page-33-26) Fascist philosophies vary by application, but remain distinct by one theoretical commonality: all traditionally fall into the far-right sector of any [political spectrum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_spectrum), catalyzed by afflicted class identities over conventional social inequities.[\[1\]](#page-31-0)

### **Position on the political spectrum**

Scholars place fascism on the [far right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far_right) of the political spectrum.[\[1\]](#page-31-0)[\[6\]](#page-32-4)[\[7\]](#page-32-5) Such scholarship focuses on its [social](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_conservatism) [conservatism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_conservatism) and its [authoritarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authoritarian) means of opposing [egalitarianism.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egalitarianism) [\[63\]](#page-34-11) Roderick Stackelberg places fascism including Nazism, which he says is "a radical variant of fascism"—on the political right by explaining: "The more a person deems absolute equality among all people to be a desirable condition, the further left he or she will be on the ideological spectrum. The more a person considers inequality to be unavoidable or even desirable, the further to the right he or she will be."[\[64\]](#page-34-12)

Fascism's origins are complex and include many seemingly contradictory viewpoints, ultimately centered on a mythos of national rebirth from decadence.[\[65\]](#page-34-13) Fascism was founded during [World War I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_I) by Italian [national syndicalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_syndicalists) who drew

## ![](_page_4_Picture_4.jpeg)

Pro-government demonstration in [Salamanca,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salamanca) [Francoist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francoist_Spain) Spain, in 1937. [Francisco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francisco_Franco) Franco was later labeled by some commentators the "last surviving fascist dictator". [\[62\]](#page-34-10)

upon both [left-wing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Left-wing) organizational tactics and [right-wing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right-wing) political views.[\[66\]](#page-34-14) [Italian fascism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_fascism) gravitated to the right in the early 1920s.[\[67\]](#page-34-15) A major element of fascist ideology that has been deemed to be far right is its stated goal to promote the right of a supposedly [superior people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supremacism) to dominate, while purging society of supposedly inferior elements.[\[68\]](#page-34-16)

Mussolini and [Giovanni Gentile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giovanni_Gentile) described their ideology as right-wing in the political essay *[The Doctrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Doctrine_of_Fascism) [of Fascism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Doctrine_of_Fascism)* (1932), stating: "We are free to believe that this is the century of authority, a century tending to the 'right,' a fascist century."[\[69\]](#page-34-17) Mussolini stated that fascism's position on the political spectrum was not a serious issue for fascists: "[F]ascism, sitting on the right, could also have sat on the mountain of the center. ... These words in any case do not have a fixed and unchanged meaning: they do have a variable subject to location, time and spirit. We don't give a damn about these empty terminologies and we despise those who are terrorized by these words."[\[70\]](#page-34-18)

Major Italian groups politically on the right, especially rich landowners and big business, feared an uprising by groups on the left, such as sharecroppers and labour unions.[\[71\]](#page-34-19) They welcomed fascism and supported its violent suppression of opponents on the left.[\[72\]](#page-34-20) The accommodation of the political right into the Italian Fascist movement in the early 1920s created internal factions within the movement. The "fascist left" included [Michele Bianchi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michele_Bianchi), [Giuseppe Bottai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giuseppe_Bottai), [Angelo Oliviero Olivetti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angelo_Oliviero_Olivetti), [Sergio Panunzio,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergio_Panunzio) and [Edmondo Rossoni,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmondo_Rossoni) who were committed to advancing national syndicalism as a replacement for parliamentary liberalism in order to modernize the economy and advance the interests of workers and the common people.[\[73\]](#page-34-21) The "fascist right" included members of the paramilitary [Blackshirts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blackshirts) and former members of the [Italian Nationalist Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Nationalist_Association) (ANI).[\[73\]](#page-34-21) The Blackshirts wanted to establish fascism as a complete dictatorship, while the former ANI members, including [Alfredo Rocco,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfredo_Rocco) sought to institute an authoritarian corporatist state to replace the liberal state in Italy while retaining the existing elites.[\[73\]](#page-34-21) Upon accommodating the political right, there arose a group of monarchist fascists who sought to use fascism to create an [absolute monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Absolute_monarchy) under King [Victor Emmanuel III of Italy.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victor_Emmanuel_III_of_Italy) [\[73\]](#page-34-21)

A number of post-World War II fascist movements described themselves as a "third position", outside the traditional political spectrum.[\[74\]](#page-34-22) [Falange Española de las JONS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falange_Espa%C3%B1ola_de_las_JONS) leader [José Antonio Primo de Rivera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Antonio_Primo_de_Rivera) said: "[B]asically the Right stands for the maintenance of an economic structure, albeit an unjust one, while the Left stands for the attempt to subvert that economic structure, even though the subversion thereof would entail the destruction of much that was worthwhile."[\[75\]](#page-34-23)

#### *Fascist* **as a pejorative**

The term *fascist* has been used as a [pejorative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pejorative), [\[76\]](#page-34-24) regarding varying movements across the far right of the political spectrum. [George Orwell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Orwell) noted in 1944 that the term had been used to denigrate diverse positions "in internal politics". Orwell said that while fascism is "a political and economic system" that was inconvenient to define, "*as used*, the word 'Fascism' is almost entirely meaningless. ... almost any English person would accept 'bully' as a synonym for 'Fascist' ",[\[77\]](#page-34-25) and in 1946 wrote that " 'Fascism' has now no meaning except in so far as it signifies something not desirable."[\[78\]](#page-34-26) Richard Griffiths of the [University of Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Wales) wrote in 2000 that "fascism" is the "most misused, and over-used word, of our times".[\[79\]](#page-34-27) *Fascist* is sometimes applied to post-World War II organizations and ways of thinking that academics more commonly term *[neo-fascist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-fascist)*. [\[80\]](#page-34-28)

Despite fascist movements' history of [anti-communism,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-communism) [Communist states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_state) have sometimes been referred to as *fascist*, typically as an insult. It has been applied to [Marxist–Leninist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marxist%E2%80%93Leninist) regimes in [Cuba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuba) under [Fidel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fidel_Castro) [Castro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fidel_Castro) and [Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vietnam) under [Ho Chi Minh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ho_Chi_Minh). [\[81\]](#page-34-29) Chinese Marxists used the term to denounce the [Soviet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) [Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) during the [Sino-Soviet split,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sino-Soviet_split) and the Soviets used the term to denounce Chinese Marxists,[\[82\]](#page-34-30) in addition to [social democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_democracy), coining a new term in *[social fascism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_fascism)*. In the United States, [Herbert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herbert_Matthews) [Matthews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herbert_Matthews) of *[The New York Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times)* asked in 1946: "Should we now place Stalinist Russia in the same category as Hitlerite Germany? Should we say that she is Fascist?"[\[83\]](#page-34-31) [J. Edgar Hoover](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J._Edgar_Hoover), longtime [FBI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FBI) director and ardent anti-communist, wrote extensively of [red fascism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_fascism). [\[84\]](#page-34-32) The [Ku Klux Klan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ku_Klux_Klan) in the 1920s was sometimes called *fascist*. Historian Peter Amann states that, "Undeniably, the Klan had some traits in common with European fascism—chauvinism, racism, a mystique of violence, an affirmation of a certain kind of archaic traditionalism—yet their differences were fundamental ... [the KKK] never envisioned a change of political or economic system."[\[85\]](#page-34-33)

# **History**

# **Background and 19th-century roots**

Early influences that shaped the ideology of fascism have been dated back to [ancient Greece.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greece) Mussolini had a strong attachment to the works of the Greek philosopher [Plato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plato). [\[88\]](#page-35-0) In October 1943, Mussolini was reported to have kept Plato's work *[Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_(Plato))* on his desk at home, and he claimed to consult it from time to time before beginning his work each day. [\[89\]](#page-35-1) The political culture of ancient Greece and specifically the ancient Greek city state of [Sparta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sparta) under [Lycurgus,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lycurgus_of_Sparta) with its emphasis on militarism and racial purity, were admired by the Nazis.[\[90\]](#page-35-2)[\[91\]](#page-35-3)[\[92\]](#page-35-4) Hitler emphasized that Germany should adhere to Hellenic values and culture – particularly that of ancient Sparta.[\[90\]](#page-35-2)[\[91\]](#page-35-3)

Plato supported many similar political positions to fascism.[\[93\]](#page-35-5) In his work *Republic* (c. 380 BC),[\[94\]](#page-35-6) he emphasized the need for a philosopher king in an ideal state.[\[94\]](#page-35-6) He believed the ideal state would be ruled by an elite class of rulers known as "Guardians" and rejected the idea of [social equality.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_equality) [\[93\]](#page-35-5) He believed in an authoritarian state.[\[93\]](#page-35-5) He held [Athenian democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Athenian_democracy) in contempt by saying: "The laws of democracy remain a dead letter, its freedom is anarchy, its equality the equality of unequals".[\[93\]](#page-35-5) Like fascism, he emphasized that individuals must adhere to laws and perform duties while declining to grant individuals rights to limit or reject state interference in their lives.[\[93\]](#page-35-5) He claimed that an ideal state would have education that was designed to promote able rulers and warriors.[\[93\]](#page-35-5) However, there are also significant differences between Plato's ideals and fascism.[\[93\]](#page-35-5) Unlike fascism, he never promoted expansionism and he was opposed to offensive war. [\[93\]](#page-35-5)

[Georges Valois,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georges_Valois) founder of the first non-Italian fascist party [Faisceau,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faisceau) [\[95\]](#page-35-7) claimed the roots of fascism stemmed from the late 18th century [Jacobin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacobin_(politics)) movement, seeing in its totalitarian nature a foreshadowing of the fascist state.[\[96\]](#page-35-8) Historian [George Mosse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Mosse) similarly analyzed fascism as an inheritor of the [mass ideology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_movement_(politics)) and [civil religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_religion) of the [French Revolution,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Revolution) as well as a result of the brutalization of societies in 1914–1918.[\[96\]](#page-35-8)

Historians such as [Irene Collins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irene_Collins) and Howard C. Payne see [Napoleon III,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleon_III) who ran a 'police state' and suppressed the media, as a forerunner of fascism.[\[97\]](#page-35-9) According to [David Thomson,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Thomson_(historian)) [\[98\]](#page-35-10) the Italian [Risorgimento](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Risorgimento) of 1871 led to the 'nemesis of fascism'. [William L](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_L_Shirer) Shirer[\[99\]](#page-35-11) sees a continuity from the views of [Fichte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fichte) and [Hegel,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hegel) through [Bismarck,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otto_von_Bismarck) to Hitler; [Robert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Gerwarth) [Gerwarth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Gerwarth) speaks of a 'direct line' from Bismarck to Hitler. [\[100\]](#page-35-12) Julian Dierkes sees fascism as a 'particularly violent form of [imperialism'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperialism).[\[101\]](#page-35-13)

Bust of the ancient Greek philosopher [Plato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plato), whose works were admired by Mussolini [\[86\]](#page-34-34)

## ![](_page_6_Picture_6.jpeg)

Depiction of a Greek Hoplite warrior; ancient [Sparta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sparta) has been considered an inspiration for fascist and quasifascist movements, such as [Nazism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazism) and quasifascist [Metaxism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metaxism)[\[87\]](#page-34-35)

[Marcus Garvey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcus_Garvey), founder and leader of the [Universal Negro Improvement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_Negro_Improvement_Association) [Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_Negro_Improvement_Association), had described the organisation as "the first fascists".[\[102\]](#page-35-14) In 1938, [C. L. R. James](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C._L._R._James) wrote "all the things that Hitler was to do so well later, Marcus Garvey was doing in 1920 and 1921".[\[103\]](#page-35-15)

# **Fin de siècle era and lead up to World War I (1880–1914)**

The historian [Zeev Sternhell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zeev_Sternhell) has traced the ideological roots of fascism back to the 1880s and in particular to the *[fin de siècle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fin_de_si%C3%A8cle)* theme of that time.[\[104\]](#page-35-16) The theme was based on a revolt against [materialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Materialism), [rationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rationalism), [positivism,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Positivism) [bourgeois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bourgeois) society, and [democracy.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy) [\[105\]](#page-35-17) The *fin-de-siècle* generation supported [emotionalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emotionalism_(disorder)), [irrationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irrationalism), [subjectivism,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subjectivism) and [vitalism.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vitalism) [\[106\]](#page-35-18) They regarded civilization as being in crisis, and as requiring a massive and total solution.[\[105\]](#page-35-17) Their intellectual school considered the individual as only one part of the larger collectivity, which should not be viewed as a numerical sum of atomized individuals.[\[105\]](#page-35-17) They condemned the rationalistic, [liberal individualism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_individualism) of society and the dissolution of social links in bourgeois society. [\[105\]](#page-35-17)

The *fin-de-siècle* outlook was influenced by various intellectual developments, including [Darwinian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darwinian) biology, *[Gesamtkunstwerk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gesamtkunstwerk)*, [Arthur de Gobineau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_de_Gobineau)'s racialism, [Gustave Le Bon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gustave_Le_Bon)'s [psychology,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychology) and the philosophies of [Friedrich Nietzsche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friedrich_Nietzsche), [Fyodor Dostoyevsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fyodor_Dostoyevsky), and [Henri Bergson.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henri_Bergson) [\[107\]](#page-35-19) Social Darwinism, which gained widespread acceptance, made no distinction between physical and social life, and viewed the human condition as an unceasing struggle to achieve the [survival of the fittest.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Survival_of_the_fittest) [\[107\]](#page-35-19) It challenged positivism's claim of deliberate and rational choice as the determining behaviour of humans, with social Darwinism focusing on heredity, race, and environment.[\[107\]](#page-35-19) Its emphasis on [biogroup](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/biogroup) identity and the role of organic relations within societies fostered the legitimacy and appeal of nationalism.[\[108\]](#page-35-20) New theories of social and political psychology rejected the notion of human behaviour being governed by rational choice and instead claimed that emotion was more influential in political issues than reason.[\[107\]](#page-35-19) Nietzsche's argument that "God is dead", coinciding with his attack on the "[herd mentality"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herd_mentality) of [Christianity,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity) on democracy, and on modern [collectivism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collectivism_and_individualism), his concept of the *[Übermensch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%9Cbermensch)*, and his advocacy of the [will to power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Will_to_power) as a primordial instinct, were major influences upon many of the *fin-desiècle* generation.[\[109\]](#page-35-21) Bergson's claim of the existence of an *élan vital*, or vital instinct, centred upon free choice and rejected the processes of materialism and determinism; this challenged [Marxism.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marxism) [\[110\]](#page-35-22)

In his work *The Ruling Class* (1896), [Gaetano Mosca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaetano_Mosca) developed the theory that claims that in all societies an "organized minority" would dominate and rule over an "disorganized majority",[\[111\]](#page-35-23) stating that there are only two classes in society, "the governing" (the organized minority) and "the governed" (the disorganized majority).[\[112\]](#page-35-24) He claims that the organized nature of the organized minority makes it irresistible to any individual of the disorganized majority. [\[112\]](#page-35-24)

[French nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_nationalist) and [reactionary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reactionary) monarchist [Charles Maurras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Maurras) influenced fascism.[\[113\]](#page-35-25) Maurras promoted what he called [integral nationalism,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Integral_nationalism) which called for the organic unity of a nation, and insisted that a powerful monarch was an ideal leader of a nation. Maurras distrusted what he considered the democratic mystification of the [popular will](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_will) that created an impersonal collective subject.[\[113\]](#page-35-25) He claimed that a powerful monarch was a personified sovereign who could exercise authority to unite a nation's people.[\[113\]](#page-35-25) Fascists idealized Maurras' integral nationalism, but modified into a modernized revolutionary form - devoid of Maurras' [monarchism.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monarchism) [\[113\]](#page-35-25)

## ![](_page_7_Picture_4.jpeg)

French revolutionary [syndicalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syndicalist) [Georges Sorel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georges_Sorel) (1847-1922) promoted the legitimacy of [political violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_violence) in his work *[Reflections on Violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reflections_on_Violence)* (1908) and in other works in which he advocated radical syndicalist action to achieve a revolution to overthrow capitalism and the bourgeoisie through a [general strike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_strike). [\[114\]](#page-35-26) In *Reflections on Violence*, Sorel emphasized need for a revolutionary [political religion.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_religion) [\[115\]](#page-35-27) Also in his work *The Illusions of Progress* (1908), Sorel denounced democracy as reactionary, stating that "nothing is more aristocratic than democracy".[\[116\]](#page-35-28) By 1909, after the failure of a syndicalist general strike in France, Sorel and his supporters left the radical left and went to the radical right, where they sought to merge militant Catholicism and French patriotism with their views—advocating anti-republican Christian French patriots as ideal revolutionaries.[\[117\]](#page-35-29) Initially, Sorel had officially been a [revisionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revisionism_(Marxism)) of Marxism, but by 1910 he had announced his abandonment of socialist literature. In 1914, using an aphorism of [Benedetto Croce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benedetto_Croce), he claimed that "socialism is dead" because of the "decomposition of Marxism".[\[118\]](#page-35-31) Sorel began to support reactionary Maurrassian nationalism beginning in 1909, and this influenced his works.[\[118\]](#page-35-31) Maurras held interest in merging his nationalist ideals with Sorelian [syndicalism,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syndicalism) known as [Sorelianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sorelianism), as a means to confront democracy. [\[119\]](#page-35-32) Maurras stated, "A socialism liberated from the democratic and cosmopolitan element fits nationalism well as a well made glove fits a beautiful hand."[\[120\]](#page-35-33)

The fusion of Maurrassian nationalism and Sorelian syndicalism influenced radical Italian nationalist [Enrico Corradini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enrico_Corradini) (1865-1931).[\[121\]](#page-35-34) Corradini spoke of the need for a nationalist-syndicalist movement, led by elitist aristocrats and anti-democrats who shared a revolutionary syndicalist commitment to direct action and a willingness to fight.[\[121\]](#page-35-34) Corradini spoke of Italy as being a "proletarian nation" that needed to pursue imperialism in order to challenge the ["plutocratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plutocratic)" French and British.[\[122\]](#page-35-35) Corradini's views were part of a wider set of perceptions within the right-wing Italian Nationalist Association (ANI, founded in 1910), which claimed that Italy's economic backwardness was caused by corruption in its political class, liberalism, and division caused by "ignoble socialism".[\[122\]](#page-35-35)

The ANI had ties and influence among [conservatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservatives), Catholics, and the [business community.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Business_sector) [\[123\]](#page-35-36) Italian national syndicalists held a common set of principles: the rejection of bourgeois values, democracy, liberalism, Marxism, [internationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internationalism_(politics)), and [pacifism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacifism), and the promotion of [heroism,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heroism) vitalism, and violence.[\[124\]](#page-35-37) The ANI claimed that liberal democracy was no longer compatible with the modern world, and advocated a strong state and imperialism. They believed that humans are naturally predatory, and that nations are in a constant struggle in which only the strongest would survive.[\[125\]](#page-35-38)

[Futurism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Futurism) was both an artistic-cultural movement and initially a political movement in Italy led by [Filippo Tommaso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filippo_Tommaso_Marinetti) [Marinetti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filippo_Tommaso_Marinetti) (1876-1944) who wrote the [Manifesto of Futurism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manifesto_of_Futurism) (1908), that championed the causes of modernism, action, and political violence as necessary elements of politics while denouncing liberalism and parliamentary politics.[\[127\]](#page-35-39)[\[128\]](#page-36-0) Marinetti rejected conventional democracy - based on majority rule and egalitarianism - for a new form of democracy, promoting what he described in his work "The Futurist Conception of Democracy" as the following: "We are therefore able to give the directions to create and to dismantle to numbers, to quantity, to the mass, for with us number, quantity and mass will never be—as they are in Germany and Russia—the number, quantity and mass of mediocre men, incapable and indecisive."[\[129\]](#page-36-1)

## ![](_page_8_Picture_4.jpeg)

Filippo [Tommaso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filippo_Tommaso_Marinetti) Marinetti, Italian modernist author of the [Futurist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Futurist_Manifesto) [Manifesto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Futurist_Manifesto) (1909) and later the co-author of the Fascist [Manifesto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fascist_Manifesto) (1919) [\[126\]](#page-35-30)

Futurism influenced fascism in its emphasis on recognizing the virile nature of violent action and war as necessities of modern civilization.[\[130\]](#page-36-2) Marinetti promoted the need of physical training of young men, saying that, in male education, gymnastics should take precedence over books. He advocated segregation of the genders because womanly sensibility must not enter men's education, which he claimed must be "lively, bellicose, muscular and violently dynamic".[\[131\]](#page-36-3)

### **World War I and its aftermath (1914–1929)**

## ![](_page_9_Picture_1.jpeg)

Benito [Mussolini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benito_Mussolini) in 1917 as an Italian soldier in [World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_I) War I

At the outbreak of World War I in August 1914, the Italian political left became severely split over its position on the war. The [Italian Socialist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Socialist_Party) [Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Socialist_Party) (PSI) opposed the war but a number of Italian revolutionary syndicalists supported war against Germany and Austria-Hungary on the grounds that their reactionary regimes had to be defeated to ensure the success of socialism.[\[132\]](#page-36-4) Angelo Oliviero Olivetti formed a prointerventionist *[fascio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fascio)* called the [Revolutionary Fasces of International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fascio_Rivoluzionario_d%27Azione_Internazionalista) [Action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fascio_Rivoluzionario_d%27Azione_Internazionalista) in October 1914.[\[132\]](#page-36-4) Benito Mussolini upon being expelled from his position as chief editor of the PSI's newspaper *[Avanti!](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avanti!_(Italian_newspaper))* for his anti-German stance, joined the interventionist cause in a separate *fascio*. [\[133\]](#page-36-5) The term "fascism" was first used in 1915 by members of Mussolini's movement, the Fasces of Revolutionary Action.[\[134\]](#page-36-6)

The first meeting of the Fasces of Revolutionary Action was held on 24 January 1915[\[135\]](#page-36-7) when Mussolini declared that it was necessary for Europe to resolve its national problems—including national borders of Italy and elsewhere "for the ideals of justice and liberty for which oppressed peoples must acquire the right to belong to those national communities from which they descended."[\[135\]](#page-36-7) Attempts to hold mass meetings were ineffective and the organization was regularly harassed by government authorities and socialists.[\[136\]](#page-36-8)

Similar political ideas arose in Germany after the outbreak of the war. German sociologist [Johann Plenge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johann_Plenge) spoke of the rise of a "National Socialism" in Germany within what he termed the "ideas of 1914" that were a declaration of war against the "ideas of 1789" (the French Revolution).[\[137\]](#page-36-9) According to Plenge, the "ideas of 1789"—such as the rights of man, democracy, individualism and liberalism—were being rejected in favor of "the ideas of 1914" that included "German values" of duty, discipline, law and order. [\[137\]](#page-36-9) Plenge believed that racial solidarity (*Volksgemeinschaft*) would replace class division and that "racial comrades" would unite to create a socialist society in the struggle of "proletarian" Germany against "capitalist" Britain.[\[137\]](#page-36-9) He believed that the [Spirit of 1914](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spirit_of_1914) manifested itself in the concept of the People's League of National Socialism.[\[138\]](#page-36-10) This National Socialism was a form of [state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_socialism) [socialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_socialism) that rejected the "idea of boundless freedom" and promoted an economy that would serve the whole of Germany under the leadership of

## ![](_page_9_Picture_7.jpeg)

## Adolf Hitler as a German soldier in World War I

the state.[\[138\]](#page-36-10) This National Socialism was opposed to capitalism because of the components that were against "the national interest" of Germany but insisted that National Socialism would strive for greater efficiency in the economy. [\[138\]](#page-36-10) Plenge advocated an authoritarian rational ruling elite to develop National Socialism through a hierarchical [technocratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Technocracy) state.[\[139\]](#page-36-11)

#### **Impact of World War I**

Fascists viewed World War I as bringing revolutionary changes in the nature of war, society, the state and technology, as the advent of [total war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Total_war) and mass mobilization had broken down the distinction between civilian and combatant, as civilians had become a critical part in economic production for the war effort and thus arose a "military citizenship" in which all citizens were involved to the military in some manner during the war. [\[9\]](#page-32-7) World War I had resulted in the rise of a powerful state capable of mobilizing millions of people to serve on the front lines or provide economic production and logistics to support those on the front lines, as well as having unprecedented authority to intervene in the lives of citizens.[\[9\]](#page-32-7) Fascists viewed technological developments of weaponry and the state's total mobilization of its population in the war as symbolizing the beginning of a new era fusing state power with [mass politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_politics), technology and particularly the mobilizing myth that they contended had triumphed over the myth of progress and the era of liberalism.[\[141\]](#page-36-13)

#### **Impact of the October Revolution in Russia**

The [October Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/October_Revolution) of 1917, in which [Bolshevik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolshevik) communists led by [Vladimir Lenin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Lenin) seized power in Russia, greatly influenced the development of fascism.[\[142\]](#page-36-14) In 1917,

## ![](_page_10_Picture_3.jpeg)

Members of Italy's *[Arditi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arditi)* corps, shown here in 1918 holding daggers, a symbol of their group. They were formed in 1917 as groups of soldiers trained for dangerous missions, characterized by a refusal to surrender and a willingness to fight to the death. Their black uniforms inspired those of the Italian Fascist movement. [\[140\]](#page-36-12)

Mussolini, as leader of the [Fasces of Revolutionary Action,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fasces_of_Revolutionary_Action) praised the October Revolution, but later he became unimpressed with Lenin, regarding him as merely a new version of [Tsar Nicholas II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsar_Nicholas_II). [\[143\]](#page-36-15) After World War I, fascists commonly campaigned on anti-Marxist agendas.[\[142\]](#page-36-14)

[Andreas Umland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andreas_Umland) argues that there are similarities between fascism and Bolshevism, including that they believed in the necessity of a vanguard leadership, showed contempt for bourgeois values, and had totalitarian ambitions.[\[142\]](#page-36-14) He says that in practice both have commonly emphasized revolutionary action, proletarian nation theories, one-party states, and party-armies;[\[142\]](#page-36-14) With the antagonism between [anti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-interventionist)[interventionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-interventionist) Marxists and pro[-interventionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interventionism_(politics)) fascists complete by the end of the war, the two sides became irreconcilable. The fascists presented themselves as [anti-communists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-communists) and as especially opposed to the [Marxists.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marxists) [\[144\]](#page-36-16) In 1919, Mussolini consolidated control over the fascist movement, known as *[Sansepolcrismo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sansepolcrismo)*, with the founding of the *[Italian Fasces of Combat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Fasces_of_Combat)*. [\[72\]](#page-34-20)

#### **Fascist Manifesto and Charter of Carnaro**

In 1919, [Alceste De Ambris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alceste_De_Ambris) and [futurist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Futurist) movement leader Filippo Tommaso Marinetti created ["The](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Manifesto_of_the_Italian_Fasces_of_Combat) [Manifesto of the Italian Fasces of Combat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Manifesto_of_the_Italian_Fasces_of_Combat)".[\[126\]](#page-35-30) The Fascist Manifesto was presented on 6 June 1919 in the fascist newspaper *[Il Popolo d'Italia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Il_Popolo_d%27Italia)* and supported the creation of [universal suffrage,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_suffrage) including [women's suffrage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women%27s_suffrage) (the latter being realized only partly in late 1925, with all opposition parties banned or disbanded);[\[146\]](#page-36-17) [proportional representation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proportional_representation) on a regional basis; government representation through a [corporatist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corporatist) system of "National Councils" of experts, selected from professionals and tradespeople, elected to represent and hold legislative power over their respective areas, including labour, industry, transportation, public health, and communications, among others; and abolition of the [Senate of the](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senate_of_the_Kingdom_of_Italy) [Kingdom of Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senate_of_the_Kingdom_of_Italy). [\[147\]](#page-36-18) The Fascist Manifesto supported the creation of an [eight-hour work day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eight-hour_work_day) for all workers, a [minimum wage,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minimum_wage) worker representation in industrial management, equal confidence in labour unions as in industrial executives and public servants, reorganization of the transportation sector, revision of the draft law on invalidity insurance, reduction of the retirement age from 65 to 55, a strong [progressive tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressive_tax) on capital, confiscation of the property of religious institutions and abolishment of bishoprics, and revision of military contracts to allow the government to seize 85% of profits.[\[148\]](#page-36-20) It also called for the fulfillment of expansionist aims in the Balkans and other parts of the Mediterranean, the creation of a short-service national militia to serve defensive duties, [nationalization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalization) of the armaments industry, and a foreign policy designed to be peaceful but also competitive.[\[149\]](#page-36-21)

The next events that influenced the fascists in Italy were the raid of [Fiume](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiume_(city)) by Italian nationalist [Gabriele d'Annunzio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gabriele_d%27Annunzio) and the founding of the [Charter of Carnaro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charter_of_Carnaro) in 1920.[\[150\]](#page-36-22) D'Annunzio and De Ambris designed the Charter, which advocated national-syndicalist corporatist [productionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Productionism) alongside D'Annunzio's political views.[\[151\]](#page-36-23) Many fascists saw the Charter of Carnaro as an ideal constitution for a fascist Italy. [\[152\]](#page-36-24) This behaviour of aggression towards Yugoslavia and [South Slavs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Slavs) was pursued by Italian fascists with their persecution of South Slavs—especially Slovenes

## ![](_page_11_Picture_2.jpeg)

Territories promised to Italy by the [Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_London_(1915)) of [London](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_London_(1915)) (1915): [Trentino-Alto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trentino-Alto_Adige) Adige, the Julian [March](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julian_March) and [Dalmatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dalmatia) (tan) and the [Snežnik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sne%C5%BEnik_(plateau)) Plateau area (green). [\[145\]](#page-36-19) However, after World War I, while Italy annexed the capital city [Zara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zadar) of Dalmatia the rest of Dalmatia was not assigned to Italy but to [Yugoslavia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Yugoslavia).

and Croats.[\[153\]](#page-36-25)[\[154\]](#page-36-26) The Italians claimed Fiume on the principle of self-determination, disregarding the 50.4% of its population that were [Yugoslavs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yugoslavs). [\[155\]](#page-36-27)

#### **Accommodating conservatives**

In 1920, militant strike activity by industrial workers reached its peak in Italy and 1919 and 1920 were known as the "Red Year" (*[Biennio Rosso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biennio_Rosso)*).[\[156\]](#page-36-28) Mussolini and the fascists took advantage of the situation by allying with industrial businesses and attacking workers and peasants in the name of preserving order and internal peace in Italy. [\[157\]](#page-36-29)

Fascists identified their primary opponents as the majority of socialists on the left who had opposed intervention in World War I.[\[152\]](#page-36-24) The fascists and the Italian political right held common ground: both held Marxism in contempt, discounted class consciousness and believed in the rule of elites.[\[158\]](#page-36-30) The fascists assisted the anti-socialist campaign by allying with the other parties and the conservative right in a mutual effort to destroy the Italian Socialist Party and labour organizations committed to class identity above national identity. [\[158\]](#page-36-30)

Fascism sought to accommodate Italian conservatives by making major alterations to its political agenda —abandoning its previous populism, [republicanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republicanism) and [anticlericalism,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anticlericalism) adopting policies in support of [free enterprise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_enterprise) and accepting the [Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church) and the monarchy as institutions in Italy. [\[159\]](#page-36-31) To appeal to Italian conservatives, fascism adopted policies such as promoting [family values,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Family_values) including policies designed to reduce the number of women in the workforce—limiting the woman's role to that of a mother. The fascists banned literature on birth control and increased penalties for abortion in 1926, declaring both crimes against the state.[\[160\]](#page-36-32)

Although fascism adopted a number of anti-modern positions designed to appeal to people upset with the new trends in sexuality and women's rights—especially those with a reactionary point of view—the fascists sought to maintain fascism's revolutionary character, with Angelo Oliviero Olivetti saying:

"Fascism would like to be conservative, but it will [be] by being revolutionary."[\[161\]](#page-36-34) The Fascists supported revolutionary action and committed to secure law and order to appeal to both conservatives and syndicalists.[\[162\]](#page-36-35)

Prior to fascism's accommodations to the political right, fascism was a small, urban, northern Italian movement that had about a thousand members.[\[163\]](#page-36-36) After Fascism's accommodation of the political right, the fascist movement's membership soared to approximately 250,000 by 1921.[\[164\]](#page-36-37) A 2020 article by [Daron Acemoğlu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daron_Acemo%C4%9Flu), Giuseppe De Feo, Giacomo De Luca, and Gianluca Russo in the [Center for Economic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Center_for_Economic_and_Policy_Research) [and Policy Research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Center_for_Economic_and_Policy_Research), exploring the link between the threat of [socialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialism) and Mussolini's rise to power, found "a strong association between the Red Scare in Italy and the subsequent local support for the Fascist Party in the early 1920s."[\[165\]](#page-36-38) According to the authors, it was local elites and large landowners who played an important role in boosting Fascist Party activity and support, which did not come from socialists' core supporters but from [centre-right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centre-right) voters, as they viewed traditional centre-right parties as ineffective in stopping socialism and so turned to the fascists.[\[165\]](#page-36-38) In 2003, historian Adrian Lyttelton wrote: "The expansion of Fascism in the rural areas was stimulated and directed by the reaction of the farmers and landowners against the peasant leagues of both Socialists and Catholics."[\[165\]](#page-36-38)

#### **Fascist violence**

Beginning in 1922, fascist paramilitaries escalated their strategy from one of attacking socialist offices and the homes of socialist leadership figures, to one of violent occupation of cities. The fascists met little serious resistance from authorities and proceeded to take over several northern Italian cities.[\[166\]](#page-36-39) The fascists attacked the headquarters of socialist and Catholic labour unions in Cremona and imposed forced Italianization upon the German-speaking population of [Bolzano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolzano). [\[166\]](#page-36-39)[\[167\]](#page-36-40) After seizing these cities, the fascists made plans to take [Rome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rome). [\[166\]](#page-36-39)

On 24 October 1922, the Fascist Party held its annual congress in [Naples,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naples) where Mussolini ordered Blackshirts to take control of public buildings and trains and to converge on three points around Rome.[\[166\]](#page-36-39) The Fascists managed to seize control of several post offices and trains in northern Italy while the Italian government, led by a left-wing coalition, was internally divided and unable to respond to the Fascist advances.[\[169\]](#page-36-41) King Victor Emmanuel III of Italy perceived the risk of bloodshed in Rome in response to attempting to disperse the Fascists to be too high.[\[170\]](#page-37-0) Victor Emmanuel III decided to appoint Mussolini as [Prime Minister of Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Italy) and Mussolini arrived in Rome on 30 October to accept the appointment.[\[170\]](#page-37-0) Fascist propaganda aggrandized this event, known as "[March on Rome"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/March_on_Rome), as a "seizure" of power because of Fascists' heroic exploits.[\[166\]](#page-36-39)

## ![](_page_12_Picture_5.jpeg)

Benito [Mussolini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benito_Mussolini) with three of the four [quadrumvirs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quadrumvirs) during the [March](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/March_on_Rome) on Rome (from left to right: unknown, de [Bono,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emilio_de_Bono) Mussolini, [Balbo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italo_Balbo) and de [Vecchi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cesare_Maria_de_Vecchi)) [\[168\]](#page-36-33)

### **Fascist Italy**

#### **Mussolini in power**

Upon being appointed Prime Minister of Italy, Mussolini had to form a coalition government because the fascists did not have control over the Italian parliament.[\[171\]](#page-37-1) Mussolini's coalition government initially pursued [economically liberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_liberalism) policies under the direction of liberal finance minister [Alberto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberto_De_Stefani) [De Stefani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberto_De_Stefani), a member of the Center Party, including balancing the budget through deep cuts to the civil service.[\[171\]](#page-37-1) Initially, little drastic change in government policy had occurred and repressive police actions were limited.[\[171\]](#page-37-1)

## ![](_page_13_Picture_3.jpeg)

Victor [Emmanuel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victor_Emmanuel_III) III, [King](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_of_Italy) of Italy, with Mussolini.

The fascists began their attempt to entrench fascism in Italy with the [Acerbo Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acerbo_Law), which guaranteed a plurality of the seats in parliament to any party or coalition list in an election that received 25% or more of the vote.[\[172\]](#page-37-2) Through considerable fascist violence and intimidation, the list won a majority of the vote, allowing many seats to go to the fascists.[\[172\]](#page-37-2) In the aftermath of the election, a crisis and political scandal erupted after Socialist Party deputy [Giacomo Matteotti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giacomo_Matteotti) was kidnapped and murdered by a Fascist.[\[172\]](#page-37-2) The liberals and the leftist minority in parliament walked out in protest in what became known as the [Aventine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aventine_Secession_(20th_century)) [Secession.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aventine_Secession_(20th_century)) [\[173\]](#page-37-3) On 3 January 1925, Mussolini addressed the Fascist-dominated Italian parliament and declared that he was personally responsible for what happened, but insisted that he had done nothing wrong. Mussolini proclaimed himself dictator of Italy, assuming full responsibility over the government and announcing the dismissal of parliament.[\[173\]](#page-37-3) From 1925 to 1929, fascism steadily became entrenched in power: opposition deputies were denied access to parliament, censorship was introduced and a December 1925 decree made Mussolini solely responsible to the King.[\[174\]](#page-37-4)

#### **Catholic Church**

In 1929, the fascist regime briefly gained what was in effect a blessing of the Catholic Church after the regime signed a concordat with the Church, known as the [Lateran Treaty,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lateran_Treaty) which gave the papacy state sovereignty and financial compensation for the seizure of Church lands by the liberal state in the 19th century, but within two years the Church had renounced fascism in the Encyclical *[Non Abbiamo Bisogno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non_Abbiamo_Bisogno)* as a "pagan idolatry of the state" which teaches "hatred, violence and irreverence".[\[175\]](#page-37-5) Not long after signing the agreement, by Mussolini's own confession, the Church had threatened to have him "excommunicated", in part because of his intractable nature, but also because he had "confiscated more issues of Catholic newspapers in the next three months than in the previous seven years."[\[176\]](#page-37-6) By the late 1930s,

## ![](_page_13_Picture_9.jpeg)

The signing of the [Lateran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lateran_Treaty) Treaty, Mussolini shown on the right side of the photograph.

Mussolini became more vocal in his anti-clerical rhetoric, repeatedly denouncing the Catholic Church and discussing ways to depose the pope. He took the position that the "papacy was a malignant tumor in the body of Italy and must 'be rooted out once and for all,' because there was no room in Rome for both the Pope and himself."[\[177\]](#page-37-8) In her 1974 book, Mussolini's widow Rachele stated that her husband had always been an atheist until near the end of his life, writing that her husband was "basically irreligious until the later years of his life."[\[178\]](#page-37-9)

The Nazis in Germany employed similar anti-clerical policies.[\[179\]](#page-37-10) The Gestapo confiscated hundreds of monasteries in Austria and Germany, evicted clergymen and laymen alike and often replaced crosses with swastikas.[\[180\]](#page-37-11) Referring to the swastika as "the Devil's Cross", church leaders found their youth organizations banned, their meetings limited and various Catholic periodicals censored or banned. Government officials eventually found it necessary to place "Nazis into editorial positions in the Catholic press."[\[181\]](#page-37-12) Up to 2,720 clerics, mostly Catholics, were arrested by the Gestapo and imprisoned inside of Germany's Dachau concentration camp, resulting in over 1,000 deaths.[\[182\]](#page-37-13)

#### **Corporatist economic system**

The fascist regime created a corporatist economic system in 1925 with creation of [the Palazzo Vidoni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pact_of_the_Vidoni_Palace) [Pact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pact_of_the_Vidoni_Palace), in which the Italian employers' association *[Confindustria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confindustria)* and fascist trade unions agreed to recognize each other as the sole representatives of Italy's employers and employees, excluding non-fascist trade unions.[\[183\]](#page-37-14) The Fascist regime first created a Ministry of Corporations that organized the Italian economy into 22 sectoral corporations, banned workers' strikes and lock-outs and in 1927 created the [Charter of Labour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labour_Charter_of_1927), which established workers' rights and duties and created labour tribunals to arbitrate employer-employee disputes.[\[183\]](#page-37-14) In practice, the sectoral corporations exercised little independence and were largely controlled by the regime, and the employee organizations were rarely led by employees themselves, but instead by appointed Fascist party members.[\[183\]](#page-37-14)

#### **Aggressive colonial and foreign policies**

In the 1920s, Fascist Italy pursued an aggressive foreign policy that included ambitions to expand Italian territory. [\[185\]](#page-37-15) In response to revolt in the Italian colony of [Libya,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libya) Fascist Italy abandoned previous liberal-era colonial policy of cooperation with local leaders. Instead, claiming that Italians were a superior race to African races and thereby had the right to colonize the "inferior" Africans, it sought to settle 10 to 15 million Italians in Libya.[\[186\]](#page-37-16) This resulted in an aggressive military campaign known as the [Second Italo-Senussi War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Italo-Senussi_War) also known as the Pacification of Libya against natives in Libya, including mass killings, the use of [concentration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Concentration_camp) [camps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Concentration_camp) and the forced starvation of thousands of people.[\[186\]](#page-37-16)

## ![](_page_14_Picture_6.jpeg)

Inmates at the Sid Ahmed el [Maghrun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sid_Ahmed_el_Maghrun_concentration_camp) [concentration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sid_Ahmed_el_Maghrun_concentration_camp) camp in [Libya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Libya) during the Second [Italo-Senussi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Italo-Senussi_War) War. [\[184\]](#page-37-7)

Italian authorities committed [ethnic cleansing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_cleansing) by forcibly expelling 100,000 [Bedouin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bedouins) Cyrenaicans, half the population of Cyrenaica in Libya, from their settlements that was slated to be given to Italian settlers.[\[187\]](#page-37-17)

#### **Nazi adoption of the Italian model**

The March on Rome brought fascism international attention. One early admirer of the Italian fascists was Adolf Hitler, who less than a month after the March had begun to model himself and the [Nazi Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_Party) upon Mussolini and the Fascists.[\[188\]](#page-37-18) The Nazis, led by Hitler and the German war hero [Erich Ludendorff,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erich_Ludendorff)

attempted a "March on Berlin" modeled upon the March on Rome, which resulted in the failed [Beer Hall Putsch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beer_Hall_Putsch) in [Munich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Munich) in November 1923.[\[189\]](#page-37-20)

# **International impact of the Great Depression and buildup to World War II**

The conditions of economic hardship caused by the [Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression) [Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression) brought about an international surge of social unrest.[\[191\]](#page-37-21) Fascist propaganda blamed the problems of the long depression of the 1930s on minorities and [scapegoats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scapegoat): "[Judeo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antisemitism)[-Masonic-](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freemasonry)[bolshevik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolshevism)" conspiracies, [left-wing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Left-wing_politics#Nationalism,_anti-imperialism_and_anti-nationalism) [internationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Left-wing_politics#Nationalism,_anti-imperialism_and_anti-nationalism) and the presence of immigrants.[\[192\]](#page-37-22)

In Germany, it contributed to the rise of the Nazi Party, which resulted in the demise of the [Weimar Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weimar_Republic) and the establishment of the fascist regime, [Nazi Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_Germany), under the leadership of Adolf Hitler. [\[193\]](#page-37-23)[\[194\]](#page-37-24) With the rise of Hitler and the Nazis to power in 1933, [liberal democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_democracy) was dissolved in Germany and the Nazis mobilized the country for war, with expansionist territorial aims against several countries.[\[195\]](#page-37-25)[\[196\]](#page-37-26) In the 1930s, the Nazis implemented racial laws that deliberately discriminated against, [disenfranchised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disfranchisement) and persecuted Jews and other racial and minority groups.[\[197\]](#page-37-27)

Fascist movements grew in strength elsewhere in Europe. Hungarian fascist [Gyula Gömbös](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gyula_G%C3%B6mb%C3%B6s) rose to power as Prime Minister of [Hungary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hungary) in 1932 and attempted to entrench his [Unity Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unity_Party_(Hungary)) throughout the country. He created an eight-hour work day and a forty-eight-hour work week in industry; sought to entrench a corporatist economy; and pursued [irredentist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irredentism) claims on Hungary's neighbors.[\[198\]](#page-37-28) The fascist [Iron Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_Guard) movement in [Romania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romania) soared in political support after 1933, gaining representation in the Romanian government, and an Iron Guard member assassinated Romanian prime minister [Ion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ion_Duca) [Duca.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ion_Duca) [\[199\]](#page-37-29) The Iron Guard was the only fascist movement outside Germany and Italy to [come to power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Legionary_State) without foreign assistance.[\[200\]](#page-37-30)[\[201\]](#page-37-31) During the [6 February 1934 crisis,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/6_February_1934_crisis) [France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/France) faced the greatest domestic political turmoil since the [Dreyfus Affair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dreyfus_Affair) when the fascist [Francist Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francist_Movement) and multiple far-right movements rioted *[en masse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/En_masse)* in Paris against the French government resulting in major political violence.[\[202\]](#page-37-32) A variety of [para-fascist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Para-fascism) governments
